People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I guess what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always moving from one thing to the next. Like, I just can’t idle, you know? It's funny, I was actually just trying to fix my lawnmower yesterday because of that—it’s this old Toro I’ve had for maybe fifteen years, and the spark plug keeps fouling up. So I went down to the hardware store on 4th street to get a replacement, and I ran into my neighbor Bob. He just got this new golden retriever puppy. Cute little thing, but man, those dogs will chew through your baseboards if you don't watch them... 

Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Work. I do customer service, and they just changed our whole office around. And this new work setup has just made it a lot harder to hide the fact that I'm constantly shifting around and on the go.

Yeah, I suppose I do. I really don't mean to be rude about it, but... well, at the call center, if I hear my coworkers working through a customer problem in the next cubicle, I'll just jump right in and give them the answer before they've even finished talking. Or before anyone even asked me for help, honestly. It's just right there in my head, you know? 

Like this one time last week, someone was dealing with a return on one of those heavy-duty stand mixers. My mother used to have one of those KitchenAid ones, cherry red. Thing weighed about forty pounds, I swear. She used to make these incredible snickerdoodles every Christmas with it. She always burned the bottoms just a little bit, but they were still so good. I tried making them once a few years ago and completely messed up the cream of tartar ratio. Tasted like battery acid. I don't even know why they sell cream of tartar in such big containers when you only use a pinch. 

But anyway, yeah, I'll just blurt out the solution to the customer's issue before my coworker is even done explaining it. I just can't seem to hold it in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, waiting is... it's tough. Like at work, when we have our morning team huddles or I'm in the help queue waiting for a supervisor, it makes me so antsy. I'll catch myself just refreshing my screen over and over, or I'll just want to jump in and talk before they even call my name. Speaking of names, my supervisor's name is Brenda, and she has this habit of mispronouncing everyone's name on purpose, I think. Like, she calls this guy named Julian "Julio" every single day. Julian doesn't even care anymore, he just brings in these amazing homemade empanadas on Fridays. His wife's family is from Argentina, I think. Or maybe Chile? I can never remember. I went to Chile once in my twenties, actually. Backpacked through the Andes. It was freezing, but the stars... you wouldn't believe the stars out there. No light pollution at all. 

But, uh, anyway, waiting. Yeah. I just get really restless waiting for my turn, especially in those queues. I just want to get it over with, you know?

I don't think so, no. I mean, not that I've really noticed. I don't have a whole lot of close friends right now anyway, mostly just keep to myself after work. I like to tinker with things in the garage. Lately I've been trying to fix up this old lawnmower I found on the curb a few streets over. It's a Toro, one of the older models with the red deck. They just don't make them like that anymore, you know? The metal is so much thicker. Nowadays everything is just cheap plastic that cracks if you look at it funny. Reminds me of this toaster I bought last year. Paid fifty bucks for it, and the lever snapped off after two weeks. Two weeks! I tried to glue it back on with some epoxy, but it just wouldn't hold. Epoxy is tricky like that, you have to get the ratio exactly right or it just turns into a sticky mess. I ended up getting it all over my favorite flannel shirt. 

But, uh, anyway, relationships. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm really causing any problems there. Just hanging out in the garage mostly.

Stress or anxiety? Hmm... I don't really think so. I mean, I don't feel super stressed about being on the go. I just like to stay busy, you know? Cute little thing, but man, those dogs will chew through your baseboards if you don't watch them... \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. Work. I do customer service, and they just changed our whole office around. And this new work setup has just made it a lot harder to hide the fact that I'm constantly shifting around and on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do. I really don't mean to be rude about it, but... well, at the call center, if I hear my coworkers working through a customer problem in the next cubicle, I'll just jump right in and give them the answer before they've even finished talking. Or before anyone even asked me for help, honestly. It's just right there in my head, you know? \n\nLike this one time last week, someone was dealing with a return on one of those heavy-duty stand mixers. My mother used to have one of those KitchenAid ones, cherry red. Thing weighed about forty pounds, I swear. She used to make these incredible snickerdoodles every Christmas with it. She always burned the bottoms just a little bit, but they were still so good. I tried making them once a few years ago and completely messed up the cream of tartar ratio. Tasted like battery acid. I don't even know why they sell cream of tartar in such big containers when you only use a pinch. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'll just blurt out the solution to the customer's issue before my coworker is even done explaining it. I just can't seem to hold it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in your seat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do that. Even when I’m really trying to look calm and collected, my foot is just going a mile a minute bouncing under the desk. I actually had to buy different shoes for work because my heel kept scuffing up the plastic floor mat. I usually wear those New Balance sneakers now—the grey ones, you know? They offer great arch support. My podiatrist, Dr. Schuler, told me I have flat feet and need a wide toe box. He’s a great guy, actually. Collects vintage license plates. He had this pristine 1950s Ohio plate up on his clinic wall last time I was there, the ones with the white numbers on the dark green background... \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Oh, sitting still. Right. Yeah, if it's not my foot bouncing, I’m clicking a pen during our team meetings without even realizing I'm doing it. Usually I don't stop until my supervisor glares at me. I just don't even notice it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in lines or for your turn in general?\n\nPatient: Yeah, waiting is... it's tough. Like at work, when we have our morning team huddles or I'm in the help queue waiting for a supervisor, it makes me so antsy. I'll catch myself just refreshing my screen over and over, or I'll just want to jump in and talk before they even call my name. Speaking of names, my supervisor's name is Brenda, and she has this habit of mispronouncing everyone's name on purpose, I think. Like, she calls this guy named Julian \"Julio\" every single day. Julian doesn't even care anymore, he just brings in these amazing homemade empanadas on Fridays. His wife's family is from Argentina, I think. Or maybe Chile? I can never remember. I went to Chile once in my twenties, actually. Backpacked through the Andes. It was freezing, but the stars... you wouldn't believe the stars out there. No light pollution at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, waiting. Yeah. I just get really restless waiting for my turn, especially in those queues. I just want to get it over with, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant moving from one thing to the next or interrupting others is causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it is causing some issues. It's actually started showing up in my performance reviews at the call center. My supervisors keep pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets, and I make little mistakes that end up slowing the whole team down. Which really stinks because I'm trying so hard, and I generally get along really well with the customers. \n\nLike this one lady I helped last Tuesday, Mrs. Higgins. She was so sweet, reminded me of my Aunt Martha. Aunt Martha used to knit these incredible afghans. She made me this blue and yellow one when I graduated high school. I still have it, actually, draped over the back of my couch. It's got a little hole in it from where my old dog, Buster, chewed on it when he was a puppy. Buster was a golden retriever mix, best dog I ever had. He used to love chasing those tennis balls, but only the yellow ones. If you threw a green one, he'd just look at you like you were crazy. Dogs are funny like that, aren't they? \n\nBut yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right, work problems. Yeah, the unfinished tickets and mistakes are definitely a thing, even though I'm really trying to do a good job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior is causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, not that I've really noticed. I don't have a whole lot of close friends right now anyway, mostly just keep to myself after work. I like to tinker with things in the garage. Lately I've been trying to fix up this old lawnmower I found on the curb a few streets over. It's a Toro, one of the older models with the red deck. They just don't make them like that anymore, you know? The metal is so much thicker. Nowadays everything is just cheap plastic that cracks if you look at it funny. Reminds me of this toaster I bought last year. Paid fifty bucks for it, and the lever snapped off after two weeks. Two weeks! I tried to glue it back on with some epoxy, but it just wouldn't hold. Epoxy is tricky like that, you have to get the ratio exactly right or it just turns into a sticky mess. I ended up getting it all over my favorite flannel shirt. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, relationships. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm really causing any problems there. Just hanging out in the garage mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be on the go and your tendency to interrupt others is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety? Hmm... I don't really think so. I mean, I don't feel super stressed about being on the go. I just like to stay busy, you know? Keeping moving helps me feel like I'm getting things done, even if I bounce around a bit. Like yesterday, I started out meaning to clean the gutters. I got the ladder out and everything, but then I noticed the weather stripping on the front door was peeling. So I went to the hardware store to get some new stripping, the foam kind with the adhesive backing. While I was there, they had this huge display of those solar-powered garden lights on clearance. I've always wanted to line the driveway with those. My neighbor, Gary, has them, and they look so nice at night. Gary is a retired mailman, actually. He always has the best stories about the things people try to mail. Once he said someone tried to mail a whole coconut just by writing the address on the shell with a Sharpie. No box or anything! Can you believe that? The post office actually delivered it, too. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, stress. No, I don't really feel anxious about it. I just kind of keep moving and doing my thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, looking back, this kind of thing was around long before this job change. Even way back in grade school, my teachers always wrote on my report cards that I was bright but careless. They said I talked out of turn a lot and always had to be reminded to stay with my work. 

I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was the one who wrote that the most. She had this huge beehive hairdo, I swear it was like a foot tall. She used to keep these little peppermint candies in her desk drawer, the ones with the red and white swirls wrapped in that crinkly clear plastic. I don't know why, but it seems like older folks always have those specific mints. My grandpa used to keep a handful of them in the glovebox of his old Chevy truck. That truck was a real beauty, a '67 C10, powder blue. He actually let me steer it once when we were driving down a dirt road behind his farm. We hit a massive pothole and I nearly drove us straight into an irrigation ditch! He just laughed, popped another peppermint in his mouth, and took the wheel back. They don't make trucks like that anymore, all solid steel. You could probably drive that thing through a brick wall and barely scratch the bumper... 

But, uh, what was the question again? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, my teachers definitely noticed I had trouble staying on track and keeping quiet.

Julian doesn't even care anymore, he just brings in these amazing homemade empanadas on Fridays. His wife's family is from Argentina, I think. Or maybe Chile? I can never remember. I went to Chile once in my twenties, actually. Backpacked through the Andes. It was freezing, but the stars... you wouldn't believe the stars out there. No light pollution at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, waiting. Yeah. I just get really restless waiting for my turn, especially in those queues. I just want to get it over with, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant moving from one thing to the next or interrupting others is causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it is causing some issues. It's actually started showing up in my performance reviews at the call center. My supervisors keep pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets, and I make little mistakes that end up slowing the whole team down. Which really stinks because I'm trying so hard, and I generally get along really well with the customers. \n\nLike this one lady I helped last Tuesday, Mrs. Higgins. She was so sweet, reminded me of my Aunt Martha. Aunt Martha used to knit these incredible afghans. She made me this blue and yellow one when I graduated high school. I still have it, actually, draped over the back of my couch. It's got a little hole in it from where my old dog, Buster, chewed on it when he was a puppy. Buster was a golden retriever mix, best dog I ever had. He used to love chasing those tennis balls, but only the yellow ones. If you threw a green one, he'd just look at you like you were crazy. Dogs are funny like that, aren't they? \n\nBut yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right, work problems. Yeah, the unfinished tickets and mistakes are definitely a thing, even though I'm really trying to do a good job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior is causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, not that I've really noticed. I don't have a whole lot of close friends right now anyway, mostly just keep to myself after work. I like to tinker with things in the garage. Lately I've been trying to fix up this old lawnmower I found on the curb a few streets over. It's a Toro, one of the older models with the red deck. They just don't make them like that anymore, you know? The metal is so much thicker. Nowadays everything is just cheap plastic that cracks if you look at it funny. Reminds me of this toaster I bought last year. Paid fifty bucks for it, and the lever snapped off after two weeks. Two weeks! I tried to glue it back on with some epoxy, but it just wouldn't hold. Epoxy is tricky like that, you have to get the ratio exactly right or it just turns into a sticky mess. I ended up getting it all over my favorite flannel shirt. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, relationships. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm really causing any problems there. Just hanging out in the garage mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be on the go and your tendency to interrupt others is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety? Hmm... I don't really think so. I mean, I don't feel super stressed about being on the go. I just like to stay busy, you know? Keeping moving helps me feel like I'm getting things done, even if I bounce around a bit. Like yesterday, I started out meaning to clean the gutters. I got the ladder out and everything, but then I noticed the weather stripping on the front door was peeling. So I went to the hardware store to get some new stripping, the foam kind with the adhesive backing. While I was there, they had this huge display of those solar-powered garden lights on clearance. I've always wanted to line the driveway with those. My neighbor, Gary, has them, and they look so nice at night. Gary is a retired mailman, actually. He always has the best stories about the things people try to mail. Once he said someone tried to mail a whole coconut just by writing the address on the shell with a Sharpie. No box or anything! Can you believe that? The post office actually delivered it, too. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, stress. No, I don't really feel anxious about it. I just kind of keep moving and doing my thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, this kind of thing was around long before this job change. Even way back in grade school, my teachers always wrote on my report cards that I was bright but careless. They said I talked out of turn a lot and always had to be reminded to stay with my work. \n\nI remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was the one who wrote that the most. She had this huge beehive hairdo, I swear it was like a foot tall. She used to keep these little peppermint candies in her desk drawer, the ones with the red and white swirls wrapped in that crinkly clear plastic. I don't know why, but it seems like older folks always have those specific mints. My grandpa used to keep a handful of them in the glovebox of his old Chevy truck. That truck was a real beauty, a '67 C10, powder blue. He actually let me steer it once when we were driving down a dirt road behind his farm. We hit a massive pothole and I nearly drove us straight into an irrigation ditch! He just laughed, popped another peppermint in his mouth, and took the wheel back. They don't make trucks like that anymore, all solid steel. You could probably drive that thing through a brick wall and barely scratch the bumper... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question again? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, my teachers definitely noticed I had trouble staying on track and keeping quiet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They said I talked out of turn a lot and always had to be reminded to stay with my work. \n\nI remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was the one who wrote that the most. She had this huge beehive hairdo, I swear it was like a foot tall. She used to keep these little peppermint candies in her desk drawer, the ones with the red and white swirls wrapped in that crinkly clear plastic. I don't know why, but it seems like older folks always have those specific mints. My grandpa used to keep a handful of them in the glovebox of his old Chevy truck. That truck was a real beauty, a '67 C10, powder blue. He actually let me steer it once when we were driving down a dirt road behind his farm. We hit a massive pothole and I nearly drove us straight into an irrigation ditch! He just laughed, popped another peppermint in his mouth, and took the wheel back. They don't make trucks like that anymore, all solid steel. You could probably drive that thing through a brick wall and barely scratch the bumper... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question again? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, my teachers definitely noticed I had trouble staying on track and keeping quiet.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have more problems with sitting still or paying attention than your friends when you were a kid?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Looking back, this was around long before this job change; in grade school my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, talked out of turn, and had to be reminded to stay with my work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, like I was just saying, it was definitely worse than the other kids. I mean, my teachers were always writing notes home about it. It wasn't just a new thing with this customer service job, you know? Even back in grade school, they'd say I was a bright kid, but just so careless. Always talking out of turn, always needing someone to stand over me and remind me to do my work while everyone else was just doing it. 

I remember one time we had this assignment to build a diorama out of a shoebox. I wanted to make a whole dinosaur scene, with papier-mâché volcanoes and everything. I got my mom to buy me all this clay and paint. But when it came time to actually do it, I just couldn't sit at the kitchen table long enough to finish it. I ended up gluing a couple of plastic triceratops to the bottom of a Nike box and calling it a day. Those plastic dinosaurs were pretty cool, though. I used to get them out of those quarter machines at the grocery store. You know the ones? You'd put your quarter in, turn the metal dial, and a little plastic capsule would drop down. Sometimes you'd get a dinosaur, sometimes you'd get a sticky hand that you could slap against the wall. Those sticky hands were the best until they got covered in lint and dog hair. We had this golden retriever back then, Buster. Shed like crazy. My mom was always running the vacuum...

But yeah, anyway, the other kids definitely seemed to have an easier time just sitting down and doing what they were supposed to do. I was always the one getting told to quiet down and get back to work.

in grade school my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, talked out of turn, and had to be reminded to stay with my work.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has started showing up in my reviews, with supervisors pointing out unfinished tickets and mistakes that slow the team down even though I’m trying hard and generally get along well with customers.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about 1 year and 10 months, since my job shifted into the new hybrid chat and CRM role.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be on the go and your tendency to interrupt others is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety? Hmm... I don't really think so. I mean, I don't feel super stressed about being on the go. I just like to stay busy, you know? Keeping moving helps me feel like I'm getting things done, even if I bounce around a bit. Like yesterday, I started out meaning to clean the gutters. I got the ladder out and everything, but then I noticed the weather stripping on the front door was peeling. So I went to the hardware store to get some new stripping, the foam kind with the adhesive backing. While I was there, they had this huge display of those solar-powered garden lights on clearance. I've always wanted to line the driveway with those. My neighbor, Gary, has them, and they look so nice at night. Gary is a retired mailman, actually. He always has the best stories about the things people try to mail. Once he said someone tried to mail a whole coconut just by writing the address on the shell with a Sharpie. No box or anything! Can you believe that? The post office actually delivered it, too. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, stress. No, I don't really feel anxious about it. I just kind of keep moving and doing my thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, this kind of thing was around long before this job change. Even way back in grade school, my teachers always wrote on my report cards that I was bright but careless. They said I talked out of turn a lot and always had to be reminded to stay with my work. \n\nI remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was the one who wrote that the most. She had this huge beehive hairdo, I swear it was like a foot tall. She used to keep these little peppermint candies in her desk drawer, the ones with the red and white swirls wrapped in that crinkly clear plastic. I don't know why, but it seems like older folks always have those specific mints. My grandpa used to keep a handful of them in the glovebox of his old Chevy truck. That truck was a real beauty, a '67 C10, powder blue. He actually let me steer it once when we were driving down a dirt road behind his farm. We hit a massive pothole and I nearly drove us straight into an irrigation ditch! He just laughed, popped another peppermint in his mouth, and took the wheel back. They don't make trucks like that anymore, all solid steel. You could probably drive that thing through a brick wall and barely scratch the bumper... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question again? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, my teachers definitely noticed I had trouble staying on track and keeping quiet.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with sitting still or paying attention than your friends when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was just saying, it was definitely worse than the other kids. I mean, my teachers were always writing notes home about it. It wasn't just a new thing with this customer service job, you know? Even back in grade school, they'd say I was a bright kid, but just so careless. Always talking out of turn, always needing someone to stand over me and remind me to do my work while everyone else was just doing it. \n\nI remember one time we had this assignment to build a diorama out of a shoebox. I wanted to make a whole dinosaur scene, with papier-mâché volcanoes and everything. I got my mom to buy me all this clay and paint. But when it came time to actually do it, I just couldn't sit at the kitchen table long enough to finish it. I ended up gluing a couple of plastic triceratops to the bottom of a Nike box and calling it a day. Those plastic dinosaurs were pretty cool, though. I used to get them out of those quarter machines at the grocery store. You know the ones? You'd put your quarter in, turn the metal dial, and a little plastic capsule would drop down. Sometimes you'd get a dinosaur, sometimes you'd get a sticky hand that you could slap against the wall. Those sticky hands were the best until they got covered in lint and dog hair. We had this golden retriever back then, Buster. Shed like crazy. My mom was always running the vacuum...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the other kids definitely seemed to have an easier time just sitting down and doing what they were supposed to do. I was always the one getting told to quiet down and get back to work.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid your parents or teachers ever take you to see a doctor or a specialist because of your behavior when you were a kid?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A doctor? No, I don't think so. I mean, back then, people didn't really do that for kids who just talked too much or wouldn't sit still. You just got sent to the principal's office or got a swat. 

Our family doctor was Dr. Miller, anyway. He was this older guy, he actually still made house calls sometimes when I was really little. He always carried this worn-out black leather bag that smelled like rubbing alcohol and old paper. He'd give out those lollipops with the little loop handle, you know? Saf-T-Pops, I think they were called. I always wanted the cherry ones, but my older sister, Brenda, she'd always snatch the cherry one first. She was always faster than me. Brenda lives out in Ohio now. She actually manages a bakery out there, makes these incredible sourdough loaves. She tried to send me a sourdough starter once in the mail, but it got all weird and fermented too much, and the jar actually exploded in the box by the time it got here. The post office had to put it in one of those plastic bags. It smelled like old beer for a week in my kitchen... 

Wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, seeing a specialist. No, no one ever took me to a doctor for my behavior or anything like that. Just regular checkups with Dr. Miller.

You just got sent to the principal's office or got a swat. \n\nOur family doctor was Dr. Miller, anyway. He was this older guy, he actually still made house calls sometimes when I was really little. He always carried this worn-out black leather bag that smelled like rubbing alcohol and old paper. He'd give out those lollipops with the little loop handle, you know? Saf-T-Pops, I think they were called. I always wanted the cherry ones, but my older sister, Brenda, she'd always snatch the cherry one first. She was always faster than me. Brenda lives out in Ohio now. She actually manages a bakery out there, makes these incredible sourdough loaves. She tried to send me a sourdough starter once in the mail, but it got all weird and fermented too much, and the jar actually exploded in the box by the time it got here. The post office had to put it in one of those plastic bags. It smelled like old beer for a week in my kitchen... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, seeing a specialist. No, no one ever took me to a doctor for my behavior or anything like that. Just regular checkups with Dr. Miller.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, and staying on task pretty consistently since you were a kid?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"It’s been noticeably harder to manage for about 1 year and 10 months, since my job shifted into the new hybrid chat and CRM role.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, it's always been there, like a low hum in the background, you know? But it hasn't always been this bad. It really flared up, I'd say, about a year and ten months ago. That's when they changed my job at the call center. They moved me into this hybrid role where I'm doing live chats and managing the CRM system all at once. 

It's a nightmare, honestly. The chat windows pop up with that little *ding* sound. It reminds me of the bell on the door of this corner store I used to go to when I was a teenager. Old man Henderson ran it. He sold these huge dill pickles out of a barrel. You'd use these wooden tongs to fish one out, and the brine would drip everywhere. I used to buy one of those and a cherry RC Cola on my way home from school. RC Cola is so much better than Coke or Pepsi, I don't know why it's not more popular. They used to have these glass bottles that you had to return for a deposit. I'd collect them from the neighborhood and turn them in for pocket money. Once I saved up enough to buy a leather jacket at the thrift store. I thought I looked like the Fonz. I wore that thing even in the middle of July...

But anyway, yeah, the job change. Since they switched me to that chat and CRM thing almost two years ago, the struggling to focus and stay on task has been way, way harder to manage. It's like I just can't keep all the plates spinning anymore.

Julian doesn't even care anymore, he just brings in these amazing homemade empanadas on Fridays. His wife's family is from Argentina, I think. Or maybe Chile? I can never remember. She was so sweet, reminded me of my Aunt Martha. Aunt Martha used to knit these incredible afghans. She made me this blue and yellow one when I graduated high school. Buster was a golden retriever mix, best dog I ever had. He used to love chasing those tennis balls, but only the yellow ones. If you threw a green one, he'd just look at you like you were crazy. Dogs are funny like that, aren't they? \n\nBut yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right, work problems. Yeah, the unfinished tickets and mistakes are definitely a thing, even though I'm really trying to do a good job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior is causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, not that I've really noticed. I don't have a whole lot of close friends right now anyway, mostly just keep to myself after work. I like to tinker with things in the garage. Lately I've been trying to fix up this old lawnmower I found on the curb a few streets over. It's a Toro, one of the older models with the red deck. They just don't make them like that anymore, you know? The metal is so much thicker. Nowadays everything is just cheap plastic that cracks if you look at it funny. Reminds me of this toaster I bought last year. Paid fifty bucks for it, and the lever snapped off after two weeks. Two weeks! I tried to glue it back on with some epoxy, but it just wouldn't hold. Epoxy is tricky like that, you have to get the ratio exactly right or it just turns into a sticky mess. I ended up getting it all over my favorite flannel shirt. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, relationships. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm really causing any problems there. Just hanging out in the garage mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be on the go and your tendency to interrupt others is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety? Hmm... I don't really think so. I mean, I don't feel super stressed about being on the go. I just like to stay busy, you know? Keeping moving helps me feel like I'm getting things done, even if I bounce around a bit. Like yesterday, I started out meaning to clean the gutters. I got the ladder out and everything, but then I noticed the weather stripping on the front door was peeling. So I went to the hardware store to get some new stripping, the foam kind with the adhesive backing. While I was there, they had this huge display of those solar-powered garden lights on clearance. I've always wanted to line the driveway with those. My neighbor, Gary, has them, and they look so nice at night. Gary is a retired mailman, actually. He always has the best stories about the things people try to mail. Once he said someone tried to mail a whole coconut just by writing the address on the shell with a Sharpie. No box or anything! Can you believe that? The post office actually delivered it, too. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, stress. No, I don't really feel anxious about it. I just kind of keep moving and doing my thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, this kind of thing was around long before this job change. Even way back in grade school, my teachers always wrote on my report cards that I was bright but careless. They said I talked out of turn a lot and always had to be reminded to stay with my work. \n\nI remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was the one who wrote that the most. She had this huge beehive hairdo, I swear it was like a foot tall. She used to keep these little peppermint candies in her desk drawer, the ones with the red and white swirls wrapped in that crinkly clear plastic. I don't know why, but it seems like older folks always have those specific mints. My grandpa used to keep a handful of them in the glovebox of his old Chevy truck. That truck was a real beauty, a '67 C10, powder blue. He actually let me steer it once when we were driving down a dirt road behind his farm. We hit a massive pothole and I nearly drove us straight into an irrigation ditch! He just laughed, popped another peppermint in his mouth, and took the wheel back. They don't make trucks like that anymore, all solid steel. You could probably drive that thing through a brick wall and barely scratch the bumper... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question again? I ended up gluing a couple of plastic triceratops to the bottom of a Nike box and calling it a day. Those plastic dinosaurs were pretty cool, though. I used to get them out of those quarter machines at the grocery store. You know the ones? You'd put your quarter in, turn the metal dial, and a little plastic capsule would drop down. We had this golden retriever back then, Buster. Shed like crazy. But when it came time to actually do it, I just couldn't sit at the kitchen table long enough to finish it. I ended up gluing a couple of plastic triceratops to the bottom of a Nike box and calling it a day. Those plastic dinosaurs were pretty cool, though. I used to get them out of those quarter machines at the grocery store. You know the ones? You'd put your quarter in, turn the metal dial, and a little plastic capsule would drop down. Sometimes you'd get a dinosaur, sometimes you'd get a sticky hand that you could slap against the wall. Those sticky hands were the best until they got covered in lint and dog hair. We had this golden retriever back then, Buster. Shed like crazy. My mom was always running the vacuum...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the other kids definitely seemed to have an easier time just sitting down and doing what they were supposed to do. I was always the one getting told to quiet down and get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever take you to see a doctor or a specialist because of your behavior when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: A doctor? No, I don't think so. I mean, back then, people didn't really do that for kids who just talked too much or wouldn't sit still. You just got sent to the principal's office or got a swat. \n\nOur family doctor was Dr. Miller, anyway. He was this older guy, he actually still made house calls sometimes when I was really little. He always carried this worn-out black leather bag that smelled like rubbing alcohol and old paper. He'd give out those lollipops with the little loop handle, you know? Saf-T-Pops, I think they were called. I always wanted the cherry ones, but my older sister, Brenda, she'd always snatch the cherry one first. She was always faster than me. Brenda lives out in Ohio now. She actually manages a bakery out there, makes these incredible sourdough loaves. She tried to send me a sourdough starter once in the mail, but it got all weird and fermented too much, and the jar actually exploded in the box by the time it got here. The post office had to put it in one of those plastic bags. It smelled like old beer for a week in my kitchen... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, seeing a specialist. No, no one ever took me to a doctor for my behavior or anything like that. Just regular checkups with Dr. Miller.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, and staying on task pretty consistently since you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's always been there, like a low hum in the background, you know? But it hasn't always been this bad. It really flared up, I'd say, about a year and ten months ago. That's when they changed my job at the call center. They moved me into this hybrid role where I'm doing live chats and managing the CRM system all at once. \n\nIt's a nightmare, honestly. The chat windows pop up with that little *ding* sound. It reminds me of the bell on the door of this corner store I used to go to when I was a teenager. Old man Henderson ran it. He sold these huge dill pickles out of a barrel. You'd use these wooden tongs to fish one out, and the brine would drip everywhere. I used to buy one of those and a cherry RC Cola on my way home from school. RC Cola is so much better than Coke or Pepsi, I don't know why it's not more popular. They used to have these glass bottles that you had to return for a deposit. I'd collect them from the neighborhood and turn them in for pocket money. Once I saved up enough to buy a leather jacket at the thrift store. I thought I looked like the Fonz. I wore that thing even in the middle of July...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, the job change. It's like I just can't keep all the plates spinning anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, it really is. I mean, it's frustrating because I'm trying so hard, you know? I actually really like talking to the customers, I get along with them great. But it's starting to show up in my performance reviews at work. My supervisors are always pointing out that I leave tickets unfinished or make these careless mistakes that end up slowing the whole team down. 

It's embarrassing, really. It reminds me of this time I tried to help my buddy Dave fix the carburetor on his '78 Camaro. Beautiful car, candy apple red. He loved that thing more than his own kids, I swear. Anyway, I was supposed to be keeping track of all the little screws and springs while he took it apart. I put them in this old coffee tin—Folgers, the classic red plastic one. But then I got distracted by his neighbor's dog, this huge Great Dane that was trying to dig under the fence, and I accidentally kicked the coffee tin. Screws went everywhere, down the driveway, into the grass. Dave was so mad. We spent three hours with a magnet trying to find them all. We never did find the idle mixture screw. He had to order a whole new rebuild kit. That kit took two weeks to arrive, and he had to take the bus to work the whole time. He complained about the bus smelling like wet wool and old French fries every single day... 

But yeah, the mistakes at work. It's really stressing me out because I don't want them to think I don't care. I do care, I just... I can't seem to keep it all straight.

I do customer service, and they just changed our whole office around. And this new work setup has just made it a lot harder to hide the fact that I'm constantly shifting around and on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do. I really don't mean to be rude about it, but... well, at the call center, if I hear my coworkers working through a customer problem in the next cubicle, I'll just jump right in and give them the answer before they've even finished talking. Or before anyone even asked me for help, honestly. It's just right there in my head, you know? \n\nLike this one time last week, someone was dealing with a return on one of those heavy-duty stand mixers. My mother used to have one of those KitchenAid ones, cherry red. Thing weighed about forty pounds, I swear. She used to make these incredible snickerdoodles every Christmas with it. She always burned the bottoms just a little bit, but they were still so good. I tried making them once a few years ago and completely messed up the cream of tartar ratio. Tasted like battery acid. I don't even know why they sell cream of tartar in such big containers when you only use a pinch. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I'll just blurt out the solution to the customer's issue before my coworker is even done explaining it. I just can't seem to hold it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still in your seat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do that. Even when I’m really trying to look calm and collected, my foot is just going a mile a minute bouncing under the desk. I actually had to buy different shoes for work because my heel kept scuffing up the plastic floor mat. I usually wear those New Balance sneakers now—the grey ones, you know? They offer great arch support. My podiatrist, Dr. Schuler, told me I have flat feet and need a wide toe box. He’s a great guy, actually. Collects vintage license plates. He had this pristine 1950s Ohio plate up on his clinic wall last time I was there, the ones with the white numbers on the dark green background... \n\nBut anyway, what was the question? Oh, sitting still. Right. Yeah, if it's not my foot bouncing, I’m clicking a pen during our team meetings without even realizing I'm doing it. Usually I don't stop until my supervisor glares at me. I just don't even notice it's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting in lines or for your turn in general?\n\nPatient: Yeah, waiting is... it's tough. Like at work, when we have our morning team huddles or I'm in the help queue waiting for a supervisor, it makes me so antsy. I'll catch myself just refreshing my screen over and over, or I'll just want to jump in and talk before they even call my name. Speaking of names, my supervisor's name is Brenda, and she has this habit of mispronouncing everyone's name on purpose, I think. Like, she calls this guy named Julian \"Julio\" every single day. Julian doesn't even care anymore, he just brings in these amazing homemade empanadas on Fridays. His wife's family is from Argentina, I think. Or maybe Chile? I can never remember. I went to Chile once in my twenties, actually. Backpacked through the Andes. It was freezing, but the stars... you wouldn't believe the stars out there. No light pollution at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, waiting. Yeah. I just get really restless waiting for my turn, especially in those queues. I just want to get it over with, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant moving from one thing to the next or interrupting others is causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it is causing some issues. It's actually started showing up in my performance reviews at the call center. My supervisors keep pointing out that I have all these unfinished tickets, and I make little mistakes that end up slowing the whole team down. Which really stinks because I'm trying so hard, and I generally get along really well with the customers. \n\nLike this one lady I helped last Tuesday, Mrs. Higgins. She was so sweet, reminded me of my Aunt Martha. Aunt Martha used to knit these incredible afghans. She made me this blue and yellow one when I graduated high school. I still have it, actually, draped over the back of my couch. It's got a little hole in it from where my old dog, Buster, chewed on it when he was a puppy. Buster was a golden retriever mix, best dog I ever had. He used to love chasing those tennis balls, but only the yellow ones. If you threw a green one, he'd just look at you like you were crazy. Dogs are funny like that, aren't they? \n\nBut yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right, work problems. Yeah, the unfinished tickets and mistakes are definitely a thing, even though I'm really trying to do a good job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior is causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, not that I've really noticed. I don't have a whole lot of close friends right now anyway, mostly just keep to myself after work. I like to tinker with things in the garage. Lately I've been trying to fix up this old lawnmower I found on the curb a few streets over. It's a Toro, one of the older models with the red deck. They just don't make them like that anymore, you know? The metal is so much thicker. Nowadays everything is just cheap plastic that cracks if you look at it funny. Reminds me of this toaster I bought last year. Paid fifty bucks for it, and the lever snapped off after two weeks. Two weeks! I tried to glue it back on with some epoxy, but it just wouldn't hold. Epoxy is tricky like that, you have to get the ratio exactly right or it just turns into a sticky mess. I ended up getting it all over my favorite flannel shirt. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, relationships. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm really causing any problems there. Just hanging out in the garage mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be on the go and your tendency to interrupt others is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety? Hmm... I don't really think so. I mean, I don't feel super stressed about being on the go. I just like to stay busy, you know? Keeping moving helps me feel like I'm getting things done, even if I bounce around a bit. Like yesterday, I started out meaning to clean the gutters. I got the ladder out and everything, but then I noticed the weather stripping on the front door was peeling. So I went to the hardware store to get some new stripping, the foam kind with the adhesive backing. While I was there, they had this huge display of those solar-powered garden lights on clearance. I've always wanted to line the driveway with those. My neighbor, Gary, has them, and they look so nice at night. Gary is a retired mailman, actually. He always has the best stories about the things people try to mail. Once he said someone tried to mail a whole coconut just by writing the address on the shell with a Sharpie. No box or anything! Can you believe that? The post office actually delivered it, too. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, stress. No, I don't really feel anxious about it. I just kind of keep moving and doing my thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, this kind of thing was around long before this job change. Even way back in grade school, my teachers always wrote on my report cards that I was bright but careless. They said I talked out of turn a lot and always had to be reminded to stay with my work. \n\nI remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was the one who wrote that the most. She had this huge beehive hairdo, I swear it was like a foot tall. She used to keep these little peppermint candies in her desk drawer, the ones with the red and white swirls wrapped in that crinkly clear plastic. I don't know why, but it seems like older folks always have those specific mints. My grandpa used to keep a handful of them in the glovebox of his old Chevy truck. That truck was a real beauty, a '67 C10, powder blue. He actually let me steer it once when we were driving down a dirt road behind his farm. We hit a massive pothole and I nearly drove us straight into an irrigation ditch! He just laughed, popped another peppermint in his mouth, and took the wheel back. They don't make trucks like that anymore, all solid steel. You could probably drive that thing through a brick wall and barely scratch the bumper... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question again? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, my teachers definitely noticed I had trouble staying on track and keeping quiet.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with sitting still or paying attention than your friends when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was just saying, it was definitely worse than the other kids. I mean, my teachers were always writing notes home about it. It wasn't just a new thing with this customer service job, you know? Even back in grade school, they'd say I was a bright kid, but just so careless. Always talking out of turn, always needing someone to stand over me and remind me to do my work while everyone else was just doing it. \n\nI remember one time we had this assignment to build a diorama out of a shoebox. I wanted to make a whole dinosaur scene, with papier-mâché volcanoes and everything. I got my mom to buy me all this clay and paint. But when it came time to actually do it, I just couldn't sit at the kitchen table long enough to finish it. I ended up gluing a couple of plastic triceratops to the bottom of a Nike box and calling it a day. Those plastic dinosaurs were pretty cool, though. I used to get them out of those quarter machines at the grocery store. You know the ones? You'd put your quarter in, turn the metal dial, and a little plastic capsule would drop down. Sometimes you'd get a dinosaur, sometimes you'd get a sticky hand that you could slap against the wall. Those sticky hands were the best until they got covered in lint and dog hair. We had this golden retriever back then, Buster. Shed like crazy. My mom was always running the vacuum...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the other kids definitely seemed to have an easier time just sitting down and doing what they were supposed to do. I was always the one getting told to quiet down and get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever take you to see a doctor or a specialist because of your behavior when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: A doctor? No, I don't think so. I mean, back then, people didn't really do that for kids who just talked too much or wouldn't sit still. You just got sent to the principal's office or got a swat. \n\nOur family doctor was Dr. Miller, anyway. He was this older guy, he actually still made house calls sometimes when I was really little. He always carried this worn-out black leather bag that smelled like rubbing alcohol and old paper. He'd give out those lollipops with the little loop handle, you know? Saf-T-Pops, I think they were called. I always wanted the cherry ones, but my older sister, Brenda, she'd always snatch the cherry one first. She was always faster than me. Brenda lives out in Ohio now. She actually manages a bakery out there, makes these incredible sourdough loaves. She tried to send me a sourdough starter once in the mail, but it got all weird and fermented too much, and the jar actually exploded in the box by the time it got here. The post office had to put it in one of those plastic bags. It smelled like old beer for a week in my kitchen... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, seeing a specialist. No, no one ever took me to a doctor for my behavior or anything like that. Just regular checkups with Dr. Miller.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, and staying on task pretty consistently since you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's always been there, like a low hum in the background, you know? But it hasn't always been this bad. It really flared up, I'd say, about a year and ten months ago. That's when they changed my job at the call center. They moved me into this hybrid role where I'm doing live chats and managing the CRM system all at once. \n\nIt's a nightmare, honestly. The chat windows pop up with that little *ding* sound. It reminds me of the bell on the door of this corner store I used to go to when I was a teenager. Old man Henderson ran it. He sold these huge dill pickles out of a barrel. You'd use these wooden tongs to fish one out, and the brine would drip everywhere. I used to buy one of those and a cherry RC Cola on my way home from school. RC Cola is so much better than Coke or Pepsi, I don't know why it's not more popular. They used to have these glass bottles that you had to return for a deposit. I'd collect them from the neighborhood and turn them in for pocket money. Once I saved up enough to buy a leather jacket at the thrift store. I thought I looked like the Fonz. I wore that thing even in the middle of July...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, the job change. Since they switched me to that chat and CRM thing almost two years ago, the struggling to focus and stay on task has been way, way harder to manage. It's like I just can't keep all the plates spinning anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, and staying on task are causing you a lot of personal distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, it's frustrating because I'm trying so hard, you know? I actually really like talking to the customers, I get along with them great. But it's starting to show up in my performance reviews at work. My supervisors are always pointing out that I leave tickets unfinished or make these careless mistakes that end up slowing the whole team down. \n\nIt's embarrassing, really. It reminds me of this time I tried to help my buddy Dave fix the carburetor on his '78 Camaro. Beautiful car, candy apple red. He loved that thing more than his own kids, I swear. Anyway, I was supposed to be keeping track of all the little screws and springs while he took it apart. I put them in this old coffee tin—Folgers, the classic red plastic one. But then I got distracted by his neighbor's dog, this huge Great Dane that was trying to dig under the fence, and I accidentally kicked the coffee tin. Screws went everywhere, down the driveway, into the grass. Dave was so mad. We spent three hours with a magnet trying to find them all. We never did find the idle mixture screw. He had to order a whole new rebuild kit. That kit took two weeks to arrive, and he had to take the bus to work the whole time. He complained about the bus smelling like wet wool and old French fries every single day... \n\nBut yeah, the mistakes at work. It's really stressing me out because I don't want them to think I don't care. I do care, I just... I can't seem to keep it all straight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

No, I don't think so. I mean, back then, people didn't really do that for kids who just talked too much or wouldn't sit still. You just got sent to the principal's office or got a swat. \n\nOur family doctor was Dr. Miller, anyway. He was this older guy, he actually still made house calls sometimes when I was really little. Saf-T-Pops, I think they were called. I always wanted the cherry ones, but my older sister, Brenda, she'd always snatch the cherry one first. She was always faster than me. Brenda lives out in Ohio now. She actually manages a bakery out there, makes these incredible sourdough loaves. No, no one ever took me to a doctor for my behavior or anything like that. But it hasn't always been this bad. It really flared up, I'd say, about a year and ten months ago. That's when they changed my job at the call center. They moved me into this hybrid role where I'm doing live chats and managing the CRM system all at once. \n\nIt's a nightmare, honestly. The chat windows pop up with that little *ding* sound. It reminds me of the bell on the door of this corner store I used to go to when I was a teenager. Old man Henderson ran it. He sold these huge dill pickles out of a barrel. You'd use these wooden tongs to fish one out, and the brine would drip everywhere. I used to buy one of those and a cherry RC Cola on my way home from school. RC Cola is so much better than Coke or Pepsi, I don't know why it's not more popular. They used to have these glass bottles that you had to return for a deposit. I'd collect them from the neighborhood and turn them in for pocket money. Once I saved up enough to buy a leather jacket at the thrift store. I thought I looked like the Fonz. Anyway, I was supposed to be keeping track of all the little screws and springs while he took it apart. I put them in this old coffee tin—Folgers, the classic red plastic one. But then I got distracted by his neighbor's dog, this huge Great Dane that was trying to dig under the fence, and I accidentally kicked the coffee tin. Screws went everywhere, down the driveway, into the grass. Dave was so mad. We spent three hours with a magnet trying to find them all. We never did find the idle mixture screw. He had to order a whole new rebuild kit. That kit took two weeks to arrive, and he had to take the bus to work the whole time. He complained about the bus smelling like wet wool and old French fries every single day... \n\nBut yeah, the mistakes at work. It's really stressing me out because I don't want them to think I don't care. I do care, I just... I can't seem to keep it all straight.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had trouble sitting still, interrupting others, or staying on task for as long as you can remember, even as a young child?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, looking back, it really has been around for as long as I can remember. Way before this whole job change thing. Even back in grade school, I remember my report cards always said the same thing. The teachers would write that I was bright but careless, that I talked out of turn, and they always had to remind me to stay with my work. 

I actually found a box of my old report cards in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter boots. It was this old cardboard box that used to hold oranges from Florida. My grandfather used to order a crate of them every Christmas. They were the best oranges, so sweet and juicy, not like the ones you get at the supermarket now that taste like water and have skin so thick you need a power tool to peel them. Anyway, the box was full of stuff my mom saved. There was this one report card from Mrs. Gable in third grade. She had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. I tried to copy her handwriting for a whole year, but mine always looked like a spider crawled through an inkwell. She used to keep a jar of those little cinnamon candies on her desk, the ones that are shaped like hearts around Valentine's Day. If you got a perfect score on a spelling test, you got a candy. I only ever got one, and I'm pretty sure she gave it to me just because she felt bad that I spelled 'because' wrong for the fifth time in a row...

But yeah, the teachers always said I was smart but just wouldn't focus and talked too much. So it's definitely been there since I was a little kid.

I mean, not that I've really noticed. I don't have a whole lot of close friends right now anyway, mostly just keep to myself after work. I like to tinker with things in the garage. Lately I've been trying to fix up this old lawnmower I found on the curb a few streets over. It's a Toro, one of the older models with the red deck. They just don't make them like that anymore, you know? The metal is so much thicker. Nowadays everything is just cheap plastic that cracks if you look at it funny. Reminds me of this toaster I bought last year. Paid fifty bucks for it, and the lever snapped off after two weeks. Two weeks! I tried to glue it back on with some epoxy, but it just wouldn't hold. Epoxy is tricky like that, you have to get the ratio exactly right or it just turns into a sticky mess. I ended up getting it all over my favorite flannel shirt. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, relationships. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm really causing any problems there. Just hanging out in the garage mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be on the go and your tendency to interrupt others is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety? Hmm... I don't really think so. I mean, I don't feel super stressed about being on the go. I just like to stay busy, you know? Keeping moving helps me feel like I'm getting things done, even if I bounce around a bit. Like yesterday, I started out meaning to clean the gutters. I got the ladder out and everything, but then I noticed the weather stripping on the front door was peeling. So I went to the hardware store to get some new stripping, the foam kind with the adhesive backing. While I was there, they had this huge display of those solar-powered garden lights on clearance. I've always wanted to line the driveway with those. My neighbor, Gary, has them, and they look so nice at night. Gary is a retired mailman, actually. He always has the best stories about the things people try to mail. Once he said someone tried to mail a whole coconut just by writing the address on the shell with a Sharpie. No box or anything! I don't know why, but it seems like older folks always have those specific mints. My grandpa used to keep a handful of them in the glovebox of his old Chevy truck. That truck was a real beauty, a '67 C10, powder blue. He actually let me steer it once when we were driving down a dirt road behind his farm. We hit a massive pothole and I nearly drove us straight into an irrigation ditch! He just laughed, popped another peppermint in his mouth, and took the wheel back. They don't make trucks like that anymore, all solid steel. You could probably drive that thing through a brick wall and barely scratch the bumper... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question again? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, my teachers definitely noticed I had trouble staying on track and keeping quiet.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more problems with sitting still or paying attention than your friends when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was just saying, it was definitely worse than the other kids. I mean, my teachers were always writing notes home about it. It wasn't just a new thing with this customer service job, you know? Even back in grade school, they'd say I was a bright kid, but just so careless. Always talking out of turn, always needing someone to stand over me and remind me to do my work while everyone else was just doing it. \n\nI remember one time we had this assignment to build a diorama out of a shoebox. I wanted to make a whole dinosaur scene, with papier-mâché volcanoes and everything. I got my mom to buy me all this clay and paint. But when it came time to actually do it, I just couldn't sit at the kitchen table long enough to finish it. I ended up gluing a couple of plastic triceratops to the bottom of a Nike box and calling it a day. Those plastic dinosaurs were pretty cool, though. I used to get them out of those quarter machines at the grocery store. You know the ones? You'd put your quarter in, turn the metal dial, and a little plastic capsule would drop down. Sometimes you'd get a dinosaur, sometimes you'd get a sticky hand that you could slap against the wall. Those sticky hands were the best until they got covered in lint and dog hair. We had this golden retriever back then, Buster. Shed like crazy. My mom was always running the vacuum...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the other kids definitely seemed to have an easier time just sitting down and doing what they were supposed to do. I was always the one getting told to quiet down and get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever take you to see a doctor or a specialist because of your behavior when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: A doctor? No, I don't think so. I mean, back then, people didn't really do that for kids who just talked too much or wouldn't sit still. You just got sent to the principal's office or got a swat. \n\nOur family doctor was Dr. Miller, anyway. He was this older guy, he actually still made house calls sometimes when I was really little. He always carried this worn-out black leather bag that smelled like rubbing alcohol and old paper. He'd give out those lollipops with the little loop handle, you know? Saf-T-Pops, I think they were called. I always wanted the cherry ones, but my older sister, Brenda, she'd always snatch the cherry one first. She was always faster than me. Brenda lives out in Ohio now. She actually manages a bakery out there, makes these incredible sourdough loaves. She tried to send me a sourdough starter once in the mail, but it got all weird and fermented too much, and the jar actually exploded in the box by the time it got here. The post office had to put it in one of those plastic bags. It smelled like old beer for a week in my kitchen... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, seeing a specialist. No, no one ever took me to a doctor for my behavior or anything like that. Just regular checkups with Dr. Miller.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, and staying on task pretty consistently since you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's always been there, like a low hum in the background, you know? But it hasn't always been this bad. It really flared up, I'd say, about a year and ten months ago. That's when they changed my job at the call center. They moved me into this hybrid role where I'm doing live chats and managing the CRM system all at once. \n\nIt's a nightmare, honestly. The chat windows pop up with that little *ding* sound. It reminds me of the bell on the door of this corner store I used to go to when I was a teenager. Old man Henderson ran it. He sold these huge dill pickles out of a barrel. You'd use these wooden tongs to fish one out, and the brine would drip everywhere. I used to buy one of those and a cherry RC Cola on my way home from school. RC Cola is so much better than Coke or Pepsi, I don't know why it's not more popular. They used to have these glass bottles that you had to return for a deposit. I'd collect them from the neighborhood and turn them in for pocket money. Once I saved up enough to buy a leather jacket at the thrift store. I thought I looked like the Fonz. I wore that thing even in the middle of July...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, the job change. Since they switched me to that chat and CRM thing almost two years ago, the struggling to focus and stay on task has been way, way harder to manage. It's like I just can't keep all the plates spinning anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, interrupting others, and staying on task are causing you a lot of personal distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, it's frustrating because I'm trying so hard, you know? I actually really like talking to the customers, I get along with them great. But it's starting to show up in my performance reviews at work. My supervisors are always pointing out that I leave tickets unfinished or make these careless mistakes that end up slowing the whole team down. \n\nIt's embarrassing, really. It reminds me of this time I tried to help my buddy Dave fix the carburetor on his '78 Camaro. Beautiful car, candy apple red. He loved that thing more than his own kids, I swear. Anyway, I was supposed to be keeping track of all the little screws and springs while he took it apart. I put them in this old coffee tin—Folgers, the classic red plastic one. But then I got distracted by his neighbor's dog, this huge Great Dane that was trying to dig under the fence, and I accidentally kicked the coffee tin. Screws went everywhere, down the driveway, into the grass. Dave was so mad. We spent three hours with a magnet trying to find them all. We never did find the idle mixture screw. He had to order a whole new rebuild kit. That kit took two weeks to arrive, and he had to take the bus to work the whole time. He complained about the bus smelling like wet wool and old French fries every single day... \n\nBut yeah, the mistakes at work. It's really stressing me out because I don't want them to think I don't care. I do care, I just... I can't seem to keep it all straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sitting still, interrupting others, or staying on task for as long as you can remember, even as a young child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it really has been around for as long as I can remember. Way before this whole job change thing. Even back in grade school, I remember my report cards always said the same thing. The teachers would write that I was bright but careless, that I talked out of turn, and they always had to remind me to stay with my work. \n\nI actually found a box of my old report cards in the attic a few months ago when I was looking for my winter boots. It was this old cardboard box that used to hold oranges from Florida. My grandfather used to order a crate of them every Christmas. They were the best oranges, so sweet and juicy, not like the ones you get at the supermarket now that taste like water and have skin so thick you need a power tool to peel them. Anyway, the box was full of stuff my mom saved. There was this one report card from Mrs. Gable in third grade. She had the most beautiful cursive handwriting. I tried to copy her handwriting for a whole year, but mine always looked like a spider crawled through an inkwell. She used to keep a jar of those little cinnamon candies on her desk, the ones that are shaped like hearts around Valentine's Day. If you got a perfect score on a spelling test, you got a candy. I only ever got one, and I'm pretty sure she gave it to me just because she felt bad that I spelled 'because' wrong for the fifth time in a row...\n\nBut yeah, the teachers always said I was smart but just wouldn't focus and talked too much. So it's definitely been there since I was a little kid.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
